Description
  Text
  Empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin is a combination oral extended-release product containing a sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitor, a dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitor, and a biguanide antidiabetic agent. The combination product is indicated for adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). The combination results in further mean hemoglobin A1C reductions versus either agent alone, which may help individuals achieve target A1C goals. Empagliflozin monotherapy has been shown to have cardiovascular (CV) risk-reduction benefits by reducing the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE: CV death, the first occurrence of non-fatal myocardial infarction, or non-fatal stroke) relative to placebo in adults with T2DM and concurrent CV disease. Empagliflozin has also significantly reduced the risk of cardiovascular death and hospitalization for heart failure in adults with HF and has reduced the risk of sustained decline in eGFR, end-stage kidney disease, CV death, and hospitalization in adults with chronic kidney disease at risk of progression. However, empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin combination products are not approved for these indications. While metformin carries a low risk for lactic acidosis as outlined in the prescribing information and boxed warning of all metformin-containing products, care in prescribing and monitoring can reduce this risk. First-line T2DM therapy depends on comorbidities, individual-centered treatment factors, and management needs. In individuals with T2DM with or at high risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), heart failure (HF), and/or chronic kidney disease (CKD), certain glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) or SGLT2 inhibitors with proven benefit (e.g., empagliflozin) are appropriate initial therapy, with or without metformin based on glycemic needs. In individuals with T2DM who do not have ASCVD/indicators of high-risk, the DPP-4 inhibitors, like linagliptin, are well tolerated, have a neutral effect on weight, and have an intermediate efficacy for glucose lowering with minimal risk of hypoglycemia. Empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin combination products were initially FDA approved in 2020.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Combination products containing empagliflozin, linagliptin, and metformin are used together to improve glycemic control in type 2 diabetes mellitus.
Empagliflozin: Empagliflozin is an inhibitor of sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2), the transporter responsible for reabsorbing the majority of glucose filtered by the tubular lumen in the kidney. SGLT2 is expressed in the proximal renal tubules. By inhibiting SGLT2, empagliflozin reduces the reabsorption of filtered glucose and lowers the renal threshold for glucose (RTG), and thereby increases urinary glucose excretion, improving blood glucose control.
Linagliptin: Linagliptin is a dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitor, which exerts its actions in patients with type 2 DM by slowing the inactivation of incretin hormones. Concentrations of the active, intact hormones are increased by linagliptin, thereby increasing and prolonging the action of these hormones. Incretin hormones, including glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) and glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide (GIP), are released by the intestine throughout the day, and levels are increased in response to a meal. These hormones are rapidly inactivated by the enzyme, DPP-4. The incretins are part of an endogenous system involved in the physiologic regulation of glucose homeostasis. When blood glucose concentrations are normal or elevated, GLP-1 and GIP increase insulin synthesis and release from pancreatic beta-cells via intracellular signaling pathways involving cyclic AMP. GLP-1 also lowers glucagon secretion from pancreatic alpha cells leading to reduced hepatic glucose production, and GLP-1 slows gastric emptying time. Linagliptin increases insulin release and decreases glucagon levels in the circulation in a glucose-dependent manner; GLP-1 does not increase insulin secretion when the glucose concentration is less than 90 mg/dL. Linagliptin is of benefit in patients with type 2 DM as their GLP-1 concentrations are decreased in response to a meal.
Metformin: Metformin decreases hepatic gluconeogenesis production, decreases intestinal absorption of glucose, and improves insulin sensitivity by increasing peripheral glucose uptake and utilization; insulin secretion remains unchanged while fasting insulin levels and day-long plasma insulin response may actually decrease. Metformin improves glucose utilization in skeletal muscle and adipose tissue by increasing cell membrane glucose transport. This effect may be due to improved binding of insulin to insulin receptors since metformin is not effective in diabetics without some residual functioning pancreatic islet cells. Metformin causes a 10% to 20% decrease in fatty-acid oxidation and a slight increase in glucose oxidation. Unlike phenformin, metformin does not inhibit the mitochondrial oxidation of lactate unless its plasma concentrations become excessive (i.e., in patients with renal failure) and/or hypoxia is present. Clinically, metformin lowers fasting and postprandial hyperglycemia. The decrease in fasting plasma glucose is approximately 25% to 30%. Unlike oral sulfonylureas, it rarely causes hypoglycemia. Thus, metformin demonstrates more of an antihyperglycemic action than a hypoglycemic action. Metformin does not cause weight gain and in fact, may cause a modest weight loss due to drug-induced anorexia. Metformin also decreases plasma VLDL triglycerides resulting in modest decreases in plasma triglycerides and total cholesterol. Patients receiving metformin show a significant improvement in hemoglobin A1C.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Empagliflozin: Following oral administration, peak plasma concentrations of empagliflozin were reached at 1.5 hours post-dose. Thereafter, plasma concentrations declined in a biphasic manner with a rapid distribution phase and a relatively slow terminal phase. The steady-state mean plasma AUC was 1870 nmol/hour/L and the mean Cmax was 259 nmol/L with 10 mg empagliflozin once daily. The steady-state mean plasma AUC was 4,740 nmol/hour/L and the mean Cmax was 687 nmol/L with 25 mg empagliflozin once daily. Systemic exposure of empagliflozin increased in a dose-proportional manner in the therapeutic dose range. The single-dose and steady-state pharmacokinetic parameters of empagliflozin were similar, suggesting linear pharmacokinetics with respect to time. When the combination extended-release tablet is administered with food, no change in overall exposure of empagliflozin is evident.
Linagliptin: After oral administration, linagliptin is rapidly absorbed with peak plasma concentrations occurring in 1.5 hours in healthy subjects; the absolute bioavailability is 30%. The plasma AUC increases in a less than dose-proportional manner; following a single 5 mg dose in healthy subjects, the mean plasma AUC is 139 nmol/L and Cmax is 8.9 nmol/L. When When the combination extended-release tablet is administered with food, no change in overall exposure of linagliptin is evident.
Metformin: The bioavailability of metformin is 50% to 60%. For extended-release metformin, high-fat meals increased metformin AUC by approximately 70% relative to fasting but Cmax was not affected; the administration with meals prolonged the time to peak concentration (Tmax) by approximately 3 hours; these changes do not prevent the administration of the combination extended-release tablet with the morning meal.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Specific pharmacokinetic studies have not been performed in patients with hepatic dysfunction receiving the combination product empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin.
Empagliflozin: In subjects with mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh classes A, B, and C), the AUC of empagliflozin increased by approximately 23%, 47%, and 75%, and Cmax increased by approximately 4%, 23%, and 48%, respectively, compared to subjects with normal hepatic function.
Linagliptin: In patients with mild hepatic insufficiency (Child-Pugh class A), steady-state exposure (AUC) and Cmax of linagliptin decreased by approximately 25 and 36%, respectively, compared to healthy controls. In patients with moderate hepatic insufficiency (Child-Pugh class B), steady-state exposure (AUC) and Cmax of linagliptin decreased by approximately 14 and 8%, respectively, compared to healthy controls. Patients with severe hepatic insufficiency (Child-Pugh class C), had comparable exposure of linagliptin in terms of AUC and approximately 23% lower Cmax compared with healthy subjects. These reductions in pharmacokinetic parameters did not result in DPP-4 inhibition. Dosage adjustment of linagliptin is not necessary for patients with hepatic impairment.
Metformin: The use of metformin in patients with hepatic impairment is associated with an increased risk for lactic acidosis. Thus, the combination extended-release tablets should generally be avoided in patients with hepatic impairment.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Specific pharmacokinetic studies have not been performed in patients with renal impairment receiving the combination product empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin. However, the pharmacodynamic effect of empagliflozin is reduced in patients with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) less than 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2; this combination drug product should not be administered to these patients due to the loss in efficacy. Metformin is substantially excreted by the kidney. In patients with severe renal impairment (eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2), end-stage renal disease (ESRD), or on dialysis, this combination product is contraindicated due to the greatly increased risk of metformin-induced lactic acidosis.
Empagliflozin: In patients with mild, moderate, and severe renal impairment and subjects with kidney failure/end-stage renal disease (ESRD) patients, AUC of empagliflozin increased by approximately 18%, 20%, 66%, and 48%, respectively, compared to subjects with normal renal function. Patients with moderate renal impairment and kidney failure/ESRD had similar peak plasma levels of empagliflozin compared to patients with normal renal function. Peak plasma levels of empagliflozin were roughly 20% higher in subjects with mild and severe renal impairment as compared to subjects with normal renal function. Population pharmacokinetic analysis showed that the apparent oral clearance of empagliflozin decreased, with a decrease in eGFR leading to an increase in drug exposure. However, the fraction of empagliflozin that was excreted unchanged in the urine, and urinary glucose excretion, declined with a decrease in eGFR.
Linagliptin: Under steady-state conditions, linagliptin exposure in patients with mild renal impairment [creatinine clearance (CrCl) 50 to 80 mL/minute] was comparable to healthy subjects. Compared to healthy subjects with normal renal function, an increase in the steady-state plasma AUC of 71% was observed in patients with moderate renal insufficiency (CrCl 30 to 50 mL/minute); this increase was not associated with a prolonged accumulation half-life, terminal half-life, or an increased accumulation factor and therefore was not clinically relevant. However, in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus and severe renal impairment (CrCl less than 30 mL/minute), the steady-state exposure was approximately 40% higher than that of patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus and normal renal function (AUC increase of 42% and Cmax increase of 35%). Dosage adjustment of linagliptin in these populations is not necessary.
Metformin: Metformin will accumulate in patients with increasing renal dysfunction. Increased renal dysfunction increases the risk of lactic acidosis. In patients with decreased renal function (based on measured creatinine clearance), the plasma and blood half-life of metformin is prolonged. Renal clearance is decreased in proportion to the decrease in eGFR. In patients with severe renal impairment (eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2), the steady-state metformin exposure was approximately 40% higher than that of patients with normal renal function (AUC increase of 42% and Cmax increase of 35%). Metformin is removed by hemodialysis.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Pharmacokinetic studies of empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin have not been performed in pediatric patients.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Specific pharmacokinetic studies have not been performed in elderly patients. Based on limited data of metformin in healthy elderly subjects, the total plasma clearance is decreased, the half-life is prolonged and the Cmax is increased compared to healthy younger subjects. The change in metformin pharmacokinetics with aging appears to be primarily accounted for by a change in renal function.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Gender did not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Race did not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin.
    Name
    Obesity
    Description
    Weight (BMI) did not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin.
  Text
  Empagliflozin; linagliptin; metformin products are administered orally.
Empagliflozin: The apparent steady-state volume of distribution was estimated to be 73.8 L based on a population pharmacokinetic analysis. Empagliflozin is approximately 86.2% protein bound. No major metabolites of empagliflozin were detected in human plasma. In vitro studies suggest that the primary route of metabolism of empagliflozin in humans is glucuronidation by the uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferases UGT2B7, UGT1A3, UGT1A8, and UGT1A9. The apparent terminal elimination half-life was estimated to be 12.4 hours, and apparent oral clearance was 10.6 L/hour based on population pharmacokinetic analysis. Following once-daily dosing, up to 22% accumulation relative to plasma AUC was observed at steady-state, which was consistent with empagliflozin half-life. Following a dose of empagliflozin, 54.4% and 41.2% total radioactivity is excreted in urine and feces, respectively. The majority of drug-related radioactivity recovered in feces was unchanged parent drug, and approximately half of the dose excreted in urine was unchanged parent drug.
Linagliptin: The mean volume of distribution at steady state following a single 5 mg IV dose in healthy subjects is 110 L; linagliptin is extensively distributed in the tissues. Plasma protein binding is concentration-dependent and there is a saturation of binding to DPP-4 with increasing concentration of linagliptin. Plasma concentrations of the drug decline in a biphasic manner, with a long terminal half-life of more than 100 hours related to the saturable binding. However, this prolonged elimination phase does not contribute to drug accumulation. The effective half-life is 12 hours. Linagliptin has a terminal half-life of about 200 hours at steady-state, though the accumulation half-life is about 11 hours. Renal clearance at steady-state was approximately 70 mL/minute. Metabolism is a minor pathway of elimination; a small fraction of absorbed linagliptin is metabolized to an inactive metabolite and over 90% of the drug is excreted unchanged. Eighty-five percent (85%) of an administered dose is excreted via the enterohepatic system (80%) or urine (5%) within 4 days of dosing.
Metformin: Metformin is distributed rapidly into peripheral body tissues and fluids and appears to distribute slowly into erythrocytes and to a deep tissue compartment (most likely GI tissues). The highest concentrations of metformin are found in the GI tract (10 times those in plasma) and lower concentrations in the kidney, liver, and salivary gland tissue. Metformin is negligibly bound to plasma proteins. The apparent volume of distribution (V/F) of metformin following a single, 850 mg dose is 654 +/- 358 L. Steady-state concentrations of metformin are reached within 1 to 2 days and are generally less than 1 mcg/mL. Metformin is not metabolized by the liver, and this fact may explain why the risk of lactic acidosis is much less for metformin than for phenformin (i.e., approximately 10% of patients have an inherited defect in the ability to metabolize phenformin). The drug is excreted by the kidneys, largely unchanged, through an active tubular process; tubular secretion may be altered by many cationic drugs. Approximately 10% of an oral dose is excreted in the feces, presumably as unabsorbed metformin, and about 90% of a dose is excreted by the kidneys within 24 hours. Biliary excretion does not occur. Although the average elimination half-life in the plasma is 6.2 hours in patients with normal renal function, metformin accumulates in red blood cells, which leads to a much longer elimination half-life in the blood (17.6 hours).
 
Affected Cytochrome P450 (CYP450) enzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4, Organic cationic transporter-2 (OCT2), multidrug and toxin extrusion (MATE1 and MATE2k)
Empagliflozin: Empagliflozin does not inhibit, inactivate, or induce CYP450 isoforms or UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A8, UGT1A9, or UGT2B7. The effect of UGT induction on empagliflozin exposure has not been evaluated. Empagliflozin is a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), but it does not inhibit these efflux transporters at therapeutic doses. Empagliflozin is considered unlikely to cause interactions with drugs that are P-gp substrates. Empagliflozin is a substrate of the human uptake transporters OAT3, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3, but not OAT1 and OCT2. Empagliflozin does not inhibit any of these human uptake transporters at clinically relevant plasma concentrations and, therefore, no effect of empagliflozin is anticipated on concomitantly administered drugs that are substrates of these uptake transporters.
Linagliptin: Linagliptin is a weak to moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4, but does not inhibit other CYP450 isozymes and is not an inducer of CYP450 isozymes. Linagliptin is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and inhibits P-gp mediated transport of digoxin at high concentrations. Based on these results and in vivo drug interaction studies, linagliptin is considered unlikely to cause interactions with other P-gp substrates at therapeutic concentrations. Also, in vivo studies indicate a low tendency for drug interactions with substrates of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, CYP2C8, and organic cationic transporter (OCT); dose adjustments of linagliptin are not recommended based on results of the described pharmacokinetic studies.
Metformin: Drugs that interfere with common renal tubular transport systems involved in the renal elimination of metformin (e.g., organic cationic transporter-2 [OCT2]/multidrug and toxin extrusion [MATE1 and MATE2k] inhibitors such as ranolazine, vandetanib, dolutegravir, and cimetidine) could increase systemic exposure to metformin.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      Do not cut, crush or chew the extended-release tablet; have the patient swallow tablet whole.
Administer once daily in the morning with food.
Occasionally, the inactive ingredients will be eliminated in the feces as a soft, hydrated mass that may resemble the original tablet.
Missed Dose: If a dose is missed, instruct patients to take the dose as soon as possible. Advise patients not to double up the next dose.
    Name
    Oral Administration

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Avoid use in patients with clinical or laboratory evidence of hepatic disease as there is an increased risk of lactic acidosis secondary to the use of metformin.
  Renal Impairment
  eGFR 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2 or more: No dosage adjustment is needed.
eGFR less than 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2: Do not initiate therapy.
eGFR less than 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2: Contraindicated.
 
Intermittent hemodialysis:
Use of this product is contraindicated. Metformin is dialyzable; hemodialysis will efficiently remove accumulated metformin in the case of drug-induced lactic acidosis, provided metformin is halted.
